There is no purpose in restricting the usage of the term "narayana" to signify only Narayana by restricting its power of signification as the power of signification of the terms "vrddhi" (growth) and "guna" (multiplication) is restricted for the purpose of grammatical functions to signify the second modification of vowels \[in "vrddhir…", Panini Sutra 1:1:1\] and the first gradation of vowels \["adedgunah…", Panini Sutra 1:1:2\], respectively. One sees in the lexicons that there are several words other than "narayana", which are used in many different senses. And those words, which have settled meanings, are not seen as used only in selected senses among those meanings, by restricting their power of signification.

There is a rule: a word is understood in the sense that is heard prominently in the context. Therefore, the term "narayana", when seen together with another word that derives its power of signification from the subject of restricted usage, will also have the power of signification imparted by the subject of the restricted usage. There are thousands of usages in the narratives (other than the laudatory passages) in the Mahabharata, Bhagavata Purana, etc., in which the terms Siva, Isa, Isana, Isvara, Mahesvara, etc., are used either as adjectives of Visnu, or as subjects of predicates signifying Visnu. Also, there are lexicons, which describe these terms as having a common signifying power. Although all the above terms have a common signifying power, the terms "narayana", etc., are not seen in the narratives as used in the sense of anyone else other than Narayana, either independently, or as adjectives. Lexicons also do not support such usage of the terms "narayana", etc. Therefore, the terms "narayana", etc., are specific. So, it is not possible to entertain an illogical idea that the terms "narayana", etc., may be used by restricting their power of signification as is done in the following cases. Thus, the words "barhis" (sacrificial grass), "ajya" (clarified butter used in a sacrifice) share signifying power with the words 'trna' (grass), and "ghrta" (melted butter) respectively. However, learned men apply the terms "barhis", "ajya", etc., to the consecrated grass and sacrificial melted butter etc. by restricting their power of signification. Or, lexicons use the terms "Siva", etc., in the sense of Rudra, etc., by similarly restricting their power of signification. It is not possible to apply the term "narayana" to any other deity by restricting its power of signification without its being subject to the above mentioned rule. Especially, the term "narayana" is not seen used in the sense of Siva, etc., in any passage other than the passages cited by you. The statements you cited are subject to many objections as will be shown later. Therefore, there is no possibility that the terms "narayana", etc., signify anyone other than Visnu. Therefore, it is inappropriate to argue that lexicons restrict the power of signification, and use the terms "narayana", etc., to signify Narayana. Therefore, since one cannot refute the signifying power of the term "narayana" as based upon Visnu, its use as referring to anyone else other than Narayana is certainly refuted.

If the rule "custom supersedes etymology" \["rudhir…"\] is not accepted, the words "gau" \[cow : derived from the root "Vgam" = to go\], etc., can be used to signify only the agent of an action of going, and not when the "cow", etc., is stationary. It will also be futile to think of customs.

Even if, with great etymology, all the words signifying each of the three deities are understood as signifying all the three deities, there would be an inevitable problem, in the view of the learned tradition, of arranging religious practices practised on various days of ekadasi, pradosa, or Sivaratri, etc., for different deities.

Etymology of the term "narayana" to signify any deity other than Visnu will be refuted later.

The argument, "A term signifying any one deity signifies all other deities", is based on the rule "all words signify all meanings," is inappropriate.

If this argument were accepted, there would be many problems. Thus, there are some Vedic statements, which say "Brahman is one without any other", and some say the opposite. Some statements enjoin sacrificial rituals, and some prohibit. Some declare that there is a world beyond this world, and some declare that there is no existence. All such (contradictory) statements would be perfectly valid. The different smrti texts, history, and puranas, which follow the meanings of different statements, would also be similarly valid. It would be impossible to determine the validity of any statement, on the basis of the authority of sruti, linga (the suggestive, or secondary sense of a word, or words), grammar, etc. And the Buddhists would prevail. Various tools of interpretation such as grammar, analogy, lexicons, verbal authority, proximity of the terms that are supplied to complete an elliptical sentence, which are used for comprehending the meaning of statements, would be futile. It would be impossible to determine the meaning of any statement and distinguish between any practices.

The argument that the word "dvija" (twice-born) signifies a member belonging to three castes is also improper.

The term "dvija" (twice-born) is frequently used to signify Brahmins only. Elsewhere, it is accepted in the etymological sense. It is also accepted that the statement in the Narada Purana intends to explain its etymology.

The argument that the terms "brahmana", and "smarta" refer to the Smartas, Vaisnavas, and Saivas, etc., is also not correct.

Although the attributes of being Brahmins and Smartas are present in the Vaisnavas, an additional distinguishing attribute of being Vaisnavas is also present there. Therefore, Vaisnavas are referred to by their distinguishing attribute (of being Vaisnavas) by following the "brahmanaparivrajakanyaya" \["The maxim of the Brahmins and the mendicants", Jacob, Laukikanyayanjali, p. 314\] and "gobalivardanyaya" \['The maxim of the cattle and the bull': Jacob, Laukikanyayanjali, p. 148\].

In the present context, the above example is irrelevant. There is no distinguishing factor that would be common to all the three deities.

You cited usages of interpretations explaining the term "narayana" as referring to Siva. However, these usages are not cited in the Saivite texts such as Nilakantha Bhasya, Sivarkamanidipika, Sivastutisuktimalika, Sivatattvaviveka, and Saivakarnamrta, etc., which are extremely insistent upon establishing that the term "narayana" refers to Siva. Our texts, which refute the above texts line by line, also do not cite those passages. Interpolations are seen in the texts of Mahabharata, etc. Therefore, it is inevitable to suspect the existence of interpolations in the very large puranas, such as Siva Purana, Skanda Purana, etc. The Siva Purana, Skanda Purana, etc., are generally edited only by the Saivites. Even narrations of greatness of modern temples are presented as though they belong in the Siva Purana, Skanda Purana, etc. Therefore, the usages you cited are not at all from the puranas.

It is said that Appayya Dlksita quoted a statement from the Skanda Purana. Some modem writers too must have written that in the lexicons within the last ten years. It is not seen in the previous books. Our partisans, who refuted the book by Diksita, did not cite it.

Previously, we had a debate with Mallanaradhya, etc., who are very well acquainted with the works of Appayya Diksita. They interpreted the term "narayana" as referring to Siva by analyzing it as "naranamayanau yasmat sah". However, this resolution of the compound word into its constituent parts is not based on the etymological origin as the analyses of the term "narayana" as referring to Visnu are. Therefore, the above analysis is not acceptable. When we thus found fault with their analysis, they accepted our objection. Besides, it is quite contrary in the context of the treatise by Diksita.

Therefore, those statements should not be accepted.

This is the main fault.

Or, let those statements be considered as belonging in the puranas. Even then they should not be accepted because those statements would be from the tamasa puranas. The Padmottara Purana tells us that the sattivika puranas have the authoritativeness whereas the tamasa puranas don't. It says, "matsyam kaurmam tatha laingam saivam skandam tathaiva ca. agneyam ca sadetani tamasani nibodha me", "tamasa niraya pradah sattvika moksadah proktah".

One should not say, "The Skanda Purana describes the Vaisnava puranas as consisting of the tamas quality ('yatha vedesu…'). It describes Siva as consisting of the quality of sattva, as He is without sleep, and says that Siva should be worshipped by the Brahmins. It further says that Visnu consists of tamas, as He is sleepy, and should be worshipped by the ksatriyas. Thus, the puranas contain mutually conflicting statements. Consequently, all the puranas would lose their authoritativeness. In order to avoid such a situation, it is understood that the puranas praise the subject of their narrative after ridiculing other subjects. Therefore, the authority of all the puranas is established. Therefore, the statements from the Skanda Purana also should be accepted as authoritative, and hence, it is established that with the said etymology, etc., the term 'narayana' pertains to Siva."

In that case, there would be a problem in determining in whom the sattva quality is predominant, who should, or should not be worshipped by the Brahmins, etc.

A chapter in the tenth skandha of the Bhagavata Purana narrates: "Sri Suka said, 'sarasvatyastate rajan… jahati' \[X:89:l-21\]". The above passage describes that sage Bhrgu was sent to determine who among the three gods (Brahma, Visnu, and Siva) is the most important and in whom the sattva quality is most predominant. Bhrgu visits all the three gods and comes to his decision. With it, doubts of all sages and of the world are dispelled. The passage describes Visnu as the best of all, in whom the sattva quality is predominant, and who confers salvation. Then there is a mention of liberation of all sages by resorting to the Blessed Lord. It is mentioned elsewhere also that the sattva quality is the nature of Narayana ("sattvena mucyate jantussattvam narayana tmakam").

And this is not a desirable misfortune. The cited passages from the Bhagavata Purana and the passages cited by you \["yatha devesu…" Skanda Purana\] are understood as mainly describing one God as the best among all gods. If those passages are understood as communicating about two different gods at the same time, both the passages would become meaningless.

One should not say, "Both the above passages, i.e. from the Bhagavata Purana, and the Skanda Purana) intend to declare the similarity between Visnu and Siva, who are the subjects of the descriptions."

This would be a problem because one would have to unnecessarily abandon the expressed meaning of both the passages, and both the passages would have to be understood as indicating some other meaning. It is not established that the contexts of the above passages intend to interpret those passages as indicating some other meaning. Willful misinterpretation of passages in this manner is an unwarranted stretch of rules.

Moreover, some puranas describe Narayana as the efficient material cause not different from the whole world, as immanent in all, as the support of all, as the best in all matters among gods Siva, etc., as peerless. Some other puranas say otherwise. Some puranas describe Siva and some describe the four-faced god Brahma in a similarly contradicting manner. In the sruti, only one God is described as having the above characteristics. The Vyasa Sutras also determined the same thing. It is not possible to describe Visnu, Siva, and Brahma each as having those characteristics in different kalpas because it would contradict the sruti and the Vyasa Sutras. This would create the difficulty of invalidating all the statements and phrases on which the puranas are based.

One should not say, "There is no invalidity because the descriptions of God being the material cause, etc., are meant to be from the viewpoint of the Absolute, Brahman, which is without any attributes."

If you make that argument, then those descriptions will have to be understood as not based on reality, but as laudatory statements. Also, your etymology of the term "narayana" as referring to Siva conflicts with the statements, which declare that the term "narayana" refers exclusively to Visnu. Moreover, there are no usages that are accepted by both the parties of this dispute in which the term "narayana" refers to Siva. It is not possible to explain the term "narayana" from the view-point of the Absolute (Brahman). Therefore, it is inevitable that your desired position is refuted.

The Vedas describe the material cause of the universe with statements containing the terms such as 'sat' (existent), "Brahman", "Atman", "Narayana", etc., which describe attributes. The texts of "itihasa" (history) and the puranas that augment the Vedas ("bibhetyalpa srutadvedo mamayam pratarisyati. itihasa puranabhyam vedam samupambrhatyet.") also describe the material cause of the world with statements containing terms, which describe attributes. Therefore, there is no room to imagine that the description of the material cause, etc., in the puranas is from the point of view of the unity without any attribute.

Moreover, in the view that maintains that "the description of the material cause, etc., is from the point of view of the unity without any attributes," a question arises: Is superimposition of "attributelessness" is intended? Or, the "attributeless" is indicated?

The first alternative is not correct. In all the three kinds of puranas, each of the respective deities is mainly described as the highest of all. The rest of the context of the texts relates the same meaning. If these texts are understood as describing superimposed characteristics, they will not be understood as describing the Reality. Therefore, all of these texts will have to be understood as describing what is not real. Therefore, even the texts that have unity with the injunctions ("vidhyekavakyata") in the scriptures will have to be considered as lacking authority because they have no authority to serve as evidence. Also, there is no attribute of the Attributeless, and it is not possible to superimpose any attribute upon the Attributeless.

The second alternative also is not acceptable. In the Vedic scriptures and in many Upanisads, wherever any deity is described in extraordinary terms as the "highest" of all, the terms describing that deity are used in their primary sense. In a few Upanisads, some term signifying the deity under discussion describes that deity as the highest deity. It is possible to explain such terms as used with the power of signification of the word, or with etymology, or with the power of indication. The "bahvanugrahanyaya" maintains that references in many statements will supersede references in a few. Accordingly, such terms can be understood as pertaining to the deity under discussion in many Upanisads. And by following the "cchagapasunyaya", it is also possible to explain that the Upanisads containing common words such as "sat" (that which exists), etc., are about a specific deity discussed in many Upanisads with specific terms. Therefore, all Upanisads can be established as referring to one deity. It is possible to explain that even among the puranas, which closely embrace the Vedas, the puranas that propound the deity that is established in the Upanisads as the source of the universe have the primary sense. The puranas, which refer to any other deity as the highest deity, are against the Vedas, and, therefore, have no authority, or they are merely laudatory. Thus, there is no refutation of the primary sense. Therefore, the second alternative "The Attributeless is indicated", is not possible because there is no failure of proof, or non-accomplishment of purport of the other view.

One should not say, "Only the words 'nirvisesa', 'nirguna', 'advitlya', 'neha nanasti' \[Brhadaranyaka Upanisad [IV:4:19](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bau/4.4.19) and Katha Upanisad [II:1:11](https://vedarama.com/en/books/katha-bs/2.1.11)\], etc., purport to indicate the Brahman without any attributes."

There are many terms, which describe attributes such as omniscience. The terms for the subject of a predicate also signify a subject with attributes. And there is the "bahvanugrahanyaya", as well as the "mukhyanurodhitvanyaya" which maintains that the secondary meaning will be in compliance with the primary meaning. Attributes such as "being the material cause", etc., would force us to abandon Brahman's description as "attributeless". This would be a problem. Negation of such abandonment is desirable.

The terms "nirvisesa" and "nirguna" do not necessarily purport to indicate the Brahman without any attributes. The terms "nirvisesa" (without any attributes) and "nirguna" (without qualities) do not refer to qualities that should be shunned. The term "advitlya" (one without the second) is capable of annulling descriptions (in the srutis) such as "the Soul of all", and "the chief remainder when all is restored into one". The Mimamsakas have determined with "samanamitaracchayenena" that pronouns refer to the others of the same kind. And, therefore, the term "advitiya" (one without the second) refers to the absence of the second of the same kind. The statement "neha nanasti…" also cannot nullify the characteristics, which the sruti "yasmin patca panca janah akasasca pratisthitah" \[Brhadaranyaka Upanisad [IV:4:17](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bau/4.4.17)\] describes as belonging to the Brahman. It can be understood as nullifying independent differentiations.

If you accept the terms pertaining to Brahman as false, then with the power of indication, the terms "dvitiya" (second), etc., would be understood as referring to the real "second". This is against logical rules.

One should not say, "The absence can be established because those terms pertaining to Brahman can be considered false in the sense that they are the counterparts of the total absence grounded in the substratum created by them. That would explain the term 'advitiya' as 'the absence of the real second."

There is conflict between the existence and non-existence grounded in the same substratum. Therefore, "nonexistence" would certainly have to be real. It also would not be possible to understand the absence of the real "second". Therefore, it is not correct to understand the negation in "neha nana…" in that manner.

One should not say, "The term 'dvitiya' (second) pertains to the 'Second' that is actual."

It is better to interpret the word "dvitiya" as referring to the "second of its own kind", by following the rule established by usages such as "advitiyo narapatih" (unequalled king), etc., than to interpret it as pertaining to the "Second" that is "actual" by resorting to the power of indication that is against the rules.

One should not say, "The 'Absence' is the substratum."

If you say that, you cannot justify the explanation "the total 'absence' is grounded in the substratum created by itself " by using the power of indication.

One should not say, " In that case, let there be another accurate description of what 'false' is."

If you say that, there would be a conflict with the srutis such as "neha nanasti… ", which convey the relation of the recipient and the thing to be received.

One should not say, " Only the portion that conveys the relation of the recipient and the thing to be received may be invalidated."

It is not appropriate to invalidate a part of the meaning of a sruti when there is a possibility that it may be valid. There are many srutis such as "svabhaviki jnanabalakriyaca", "ta ime satyah kamah" etc., which convey that the characteristics pertaining to Brahman are actual (i.e. true). There will be a conflict in explaining such statements. It is not appropriate to explain those as untrue. Therefore, since there is no conforming, settled rule, the explanation, given with the use of the power of indication, is inappropriate.

Besides, suppose a big book begins with one objective and another unrelated composition gets inserted in the middle of it. With the help of the power of indication, it may even be possible to explain the unrelated composition as pertaining to the main theme. However, the puranas do not begin with the purpose of describing the Brahman without any attributes. They are mentioned as being of three kinds i.e., pertaining to Visnu, etc. Therefore, it is not appropriate to interpret them as pertaining to "the Brahman without any attributes" by resorting to the power of indication.

Besides, the proposition, "The puranas pertain to the three deities," will be breached if the puranas are understood as being about the Brahman without attributes by applying the power of indication. Furthermore, you cited statements from the Vaisnava puranas, which declare that the deity discussed in the Saiva puranas is not the object of meditation. There are also statements in the Saiva puranas, which declare that the deity discussed in the Vaisnava puranas is not the object of meditation. Some statements describe all those statements as belonging in the puranas. Some describe them as untrue. Thus, all those statements would be incongruous. If all the puranas are understood as discussing the Brahman without any attributes, you will have to understand that the one 'Attributeless' Brahman is described as being the object of meditation as well as not being the object of meditation. This would be contradictory.

One should not say, "The statements mean to say that the 'variegated Brahman' (Sabala Brahman) is not the highest."

The statements describe an individual deity among the three deities as consisting of the sattva quality and, therefore, the best, and the other deity as possessing tamasa as the predominant quality, and therefore, inferior and hence, not the object of meditation. There would be no difference among the three deities since all are mixed ("Sabala"). In all cases you would have to say that the deities are variegated and therefore, not the best.

Besides, in the case of the Brahman without any attributes, power of indication is also not possible. The rule "sakyadanyena rupena jnate bhavati laksana" states that power of indication allows something to be known as something other than what is possible. It is not possible to know the "Attributeless" without distinguishing it with "possibility". It is not possible to grasp the "Attributeless" merely from the term itself. That is against the experience. Whichever form comes to mind after hearing whichever word, you remember the object characterized by that form when you hear that word. That is the rule. When you hear the word "akasa", one grasps it as distinguished by some property such as "being the vehicle of sound". Knowledge derived from words communicates distinguishing characteristics of that which is distinguished. Therefore, the Brahman without any attributes would have to be understood as qualified by an attribute and also without any attribute. That is a problem.

One should not say, "The meaning of the terms 'nirvisesa', etc., is understood in whole, undifferentiated sense."

Brahman without any attributes is beyond the cognizance of the senses. Therefore, grasping of the entire unbroken sense also would be beyond the cognizance of the senses. Besides, there is no problem of having to dispel any doubt about whether the Brahman without any attributes is experienced, or not. Also, there would arise a problem that there is nothing to prevent such grasping of the entire unbroken sense from being the object of knowledge of its own absence. Even if you somehow accept that the meaning of the terms "nirvisesa", etc., is understood in the whole, undifferentiated sense in a sentence that has the same case- relation, there are many statements in the srutis, puranas, etc., which describe the "nirvisesa" Brahman, that are in sentences in which there is no grammatical agreement in cases. And, therefore, it is not possible to understand them as having the whole, unbroken sense.

Thus, there are many faults. We have refuted the view that the puranas are about the Brahman without any attributes. Therefore, it is inappropriate to use the "power of indication" to support your argument.

Besides, all the three kinds of the puranas refer to one of the three deities with attributes. Particular puranas describe particular deities in entirety. Description of the characteristics and acts of those particular deities cannot be construed with the Brahman without any attributes. Therefore, whole compositions would be unconnected and there would be a conflict with the principal treatment of the subject.

One should not say, "All the three kinds of puranas are valid because they proclaim the greatness of those particular deities as the best and the respectively different causes according to the difference in the kalpas such as sattvika, rajasa and tamasa. All the three kinds of puranas contain statements, which describe properties such as 'the material cause of all'. These properties describe the Supreme Brahman. Such statements also should be accepted, although reluctantly, as lauding the particular deities. Therefore, all the three kinds of puranas are about the three deities."

If it is accepted that "The Brahman without any attributes is not the subject of discussion in the puranas, and the statements referring to the properties such as 'the material cause of all' do not mean to describe the Supreme Brahman, but intend to laud the three deities", the following difficulties would ensue. Because of the conflict with the logic in your commentaries, in the Vedanta, etc., one would have to explain that those statements somehow discuss the Brahman without any attributes, and "the One without the second", and cite statements from the puranas in elaboration. This is like a blind man leading another blind man with a stick. Your commentators such as Sridhara, etc., interpreted the Bhagavata Purana, etc., as being about the Brahman without any attributes. Those commentaries would have to be understood as a talk of a mad man. The puranas would not be understood as strengthening the Vedas. In the absence of regulation of the qualities of sattva, rajas, and tamas (that are so desirable to you), equilibrium among the three deities would not be established.

Now, by following the logical rules, some puranas may be accepted as completely authoritative. It may be possible to understand the smrtis such as the "sarvavestana", which are in conflict with the logical rules, as laudatory, or as being about the immanent God, and, therefore, authoritative. However, it is not right to arbitrarily determine that the meaning of many statements in all the three kinds of puranas, which refer to the Supreme Brahman is refuted.

According to your interpretation, by following the nyaya "sandigdhe tu vakyasesat", even the srutis whose meaning is not clear may be interpreted as being about three deities as the best and the respectively different causes according to the difference in the kalpas. Some statements, which are about the Supreme Brahman in those srutis, may be understood as laudatory. But then the Brahman without any attributes, which is accepted by you, would not be understood as the goal of the Vedanta.

Thus, if the puranas, etc., are understood as being about either the Brahman without any attributes, or about the deities with attributes, by following the nyaya "tundilasurata…" (love-making by the obese), either one or the other property would be discarded. Then, like a cow tired by going and coming from one pasture to the other on both banks of a river, your thinking would not settle anywhere.

Moreover, there are many mutually contradictory religious rites seen in the smrtis, itihasa, puranas, and agamas, which negate each other. If, as in your view, they were equally important, according to the sundopasund- anyaya \[Jacob, p. 288\], they all would be invalid because of the mutual contradiction.

You should not say, "According to nahinindanyaya \[Jacob, p. 149 'Blame is not employed in order to blame something that is blameworthy, but rather to praise something other than that.'\], they are authoritative in their own particular subjects."

Then the Buddhist scriptures also, which are about their own subjects, would be valid.

You should not say, "In that case, there would be no scope for the smrtis in the Nyaya Sutras."

Refuting this argument with a big argument showing that there would be no scope for other smrtis if we accept your view, would be like a roar of the ocean. And the audumbarisparsana and sarvavestana would be optional.

You should not say, "Only the acts, which are prohibited by the srutis should be considered invalid."

Prohibition of those acts is also a form of ''nahininda…".

You should not say," The 'nahininda' nyaya is applicable only when there is a conflict between two things that are of equal strength. It is not applicable when there is a conflict between a strong and a weak matter."

If you would follow the path followed by those who know the nyayas, then we have already won.

Similarly, you cited several statements that describe Siva as the best of all, in whom the sattva quality is predominant, a brahmana, and a deity of the brahmanas, whereas Visnu as not the best, predominantly of the tamas quality, a ksatriya, and a deity of the ksatriyas. These statements are against many srutis, smrtis, itihasa, and puranas, etc., and therefore, are not authoritative. The statements from the Padmottara Purana, etc., which describe Visnu as the best, are in harmony with the srutis, etc., and therefore, are valid. Therefore, the division of the puranas into sattvika, etc., as described by the statements from the Padmottara Purana, etc., should be accepted. Accordingly, since the Saiva puranas are other than the Vaisnava puranas, one should accept that the portion that is in conflict is invalid, or it is laudatory, or one should accept that in places where its meaning as referring to Siva is refuted, it describes Siva as the Immanent God, and is thus valid. All the statements relating to Siva are not true. Therefore, on the strength of those statements, one cannot prove that the term Narayana refers to Siva.

If you say, "How can statements from the Saiva puranas, which describe the puranas as sattvika, etc., be against the srutis, etc ?"

The answer is as follows. There are statements such as "mahan prabhurvai purusassatyasyaisa pravartakah", "atha yo ha khalu va asya rajasamsassosau yoyam brahma atha yo ha khalu va asya tamasomsassosau yoyam rudrah atha yo ha khalu va asya sattvikamsassosau yoyam visnuh", in the dialogue between Krsna and Udadka in the Asvamedhika Parva of the Mahabharata ("tamo rajasca sattvam ca viddhi bhavan madasrayan. tatha rudran vasun vapi viddhi matprabhavan dvija."; "srstisthitilaya… brahma… samjnitam", "kadcittamasa rudro visnussattvagune sthitah. rajasyapi tatha brahma svangunanagunanagah"), in the Visnu Purana ("yasya prasadadahamacyutasya bhutaprajasargakarontakarl. krodhacca rudrah sthitibandhahetuh yasmacca madhye purusah parasmai", 'sambhavo rudrastamasa ca samavrtah. tvatprasadajjagaddhata rajasa ca pitamah. tvatsvarupatsvayam visnussattvena purusottamah", also, "paramatmanamlsan tamasa rudrarupinam rajasa sarvalokanam sargalllapravartakam sattvena sarvabhutanam sthapakam paramesvaram sarvatmanam mahatmanam paramatmanamlsvaram. tam drstva praha vai brahma bhagavantam janardanam", and elsewhere, "sattvena mucyate jantussatvam narayanatmakam"), and in the Bhagavata Purana ("sattvam rajastama iti prakrtergunastairyuktah parah purusa eka ihasya dhate. sthityadayo haraviritcihareti samjnah. sreyamsi tatra khalu sattvatanornrnam syuh", "narayanakalassanta bhajanti hyanasiiyavah", "rajastamahprakrtayassamasllan bhajanti vai. pitrbhutaprajesadlh sriyaiscaryam prajepsavah"). We have already written about the criterion of sattva quality used to determine who is the best God in the tenth chapter of the Bhagavata Purana. Thus, Visnu is heard as consisting of sattva, and Siva is heard as consisting of tamas in the srutis, smrtis, itihasa, Vaisnava puranas, as well as Saiva puranas. However, in the statement you cited, we hear the opposite.

Thus:

"rudram tu sarathim krtva manah pragrahavannarah. Prayati paramam param visnavakhyam padamavyayam"  
"sattvam pusannapavrnu satyadharmaya visnave. parlksya lokan karmacitan brahmano nirvedamayat."  
"tametam vedanuvacanena brahmana vividisanti." "yajnena danena tapasa nasakena."  
"yamuktva mucyate yogi janmasamsarabandhanat. onnamo narayanayeti mantropasaka. narayana- sayujyamavapnoti."  
"vedahametam purusam mahantam"  
"tamevam vidvan amrta iha bhavati"  
"nanyah pantha ayanaya vidyate"  
"nanyah pantha vidyate'yanaya" "sannyasayogadyatayassuddhasattvah" \[Mundaka Upanisad [III:2:6](https://vedarama.com/en/books/mundaka/3.2.6); Mahanarayana Upanisad X:6; Kaivalya Upanisad 3\]  
"yajno vai visnuh. Yajnenaiva yajnamayajantah devah" "moksamicchejjanardanat"  
"sarvaphalaprado hi visnuh"  
"vedaisca sarvairahameva vedyah" \[Bhagavadgita [XV: 15](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/15.15)\]  
"narayanapara veda narayanapara makhah" "vasudevapara veda vasudevapara makhah" "mamupetya tu kaunteya punarjanma na vidyate" \[Bhagavadgita [VIII:16](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/8.16)\]  
"ye'pyanyadevata bhakta yajante sraddhayanvitah. tepi mameva kaunteya yajantyavidhipurvakam" \[Bhagavadgita [IX:23](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/9.23)\]  
"akasat patitam toyam yatha gacchati sagaram. sarva deva namaskarah kesavam pratigacchati" "dhyeyah sada savitrmandalamadhyavarti narayanah "svapaco'pi mahipala visnubhakto dvijadhikah. visnubhaktivihinastu yatisca svapacadhamah" "caturbhisca caturbhisca dvabhyam patcabhireva ca huyate ca punardvabhyam sa me visnuh prasidatu"  
"vedaksarani yavanti pathittani dvijatibhih. tavanti harinamani kirtitani na samsayah."  
"pathan dvijo vagrsabhatvamiyat"  
"vedantago brahmanassyat"

The ascetics remember Visnu by saying, "yajne visnurvisnurvisnu". Innumerable statements from the srutis, smrtis, itihasa, and the puranas show that Visnu is the deity to be worshipped in all regular, and occasional acts such as sankalpa, sandhyavandana, etc., which Brahmins and all others are expected to perform, and which are related to the srutis, smrtis, etc.

Thus, there are statements: in the paitrka (sraddha, the sacred rite in honour of deceased ancestor, "Sri Visnupade dattam srijanardanah priyatam"), in sankalpa (a vow to perform any ritual observance, "Sri Govinda, Govinda, Govinda Srimahavisnorajna pravartamanasya"), in the sandhyavandana, etc. (religious acts performed by the brahmins, "kesava…" etc.), at the beginning and at the end of Vedic recitation: "harih om", during the wedding ceremony, ("dasyami visnave tubhyam…").

When the sages debated about the sattva quality of the three deities, we heard that Brahmins should worship Visnu. It seems that the statement you cited says that Visnu should be worshipped by the ksatriyas, and Brahmins should worship Siva. This is against everything cited above. Similarly, to say that "Visnu is ksatriya and Siva is Brahmin," is also full of contradiction. The sruti "brahmanasya mukhamasit" \[Rgveda X:90:13\] and its elaborations describe human beings as Brahmins. Gods do not have any such characteristics.

You should not say," The astronomy texts (one of the six kinds of Vedangas) mention that the planets are Brahmins, etc."

Such characteristics are assigned for getting some fruits, just as some planets are thought of as females even though they are males. The statement that says that "Visnu creates delusion in his devotees", is also against many statements in the srutis, smrtis, sutras, itihasa, and the puranas, which describe Visnu as the friend of all, free from partiality and cruelty, loving to those who seek refuge in Him, always attentive to the needs of His devotees, etc., "yamevaisa vrnute tena labhyah", "mata pita bhrata nivasassaranam suhrt", "gatirnarayanahu", "bhaktya tvananyayaasakyah" \[Bhagavadgita [XI:54](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/11.54)\], "bhaktya mamabhijanati" \[Bhagavadgita [XVIII:55](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/18.55)\], "bhaktiprito janardanah", "bhaktanamanukampaya-", "paritranaya sadhunam" \[Bhagavadgita [IV:8](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/4.8)\], "jnani tvatmaiva me matam", "bhaktavatsalah", "lokanugrahakamyaya", "suhrdam sarvabhutanam-", "mama madbhaktesu pritirabhyadhika bhavet", "anyatpurnadapam kumbhadanyatpadavanejanat anyatkusalasamprasnanna cecchati janardanah", "mitrabhavena sampraptam na tyajeyam kathatcana", "viditassa hi dharmajnassaranagatavatsalah", "raksyapeksam pratiksate", "sakrdeva prapannaya tavasmiti ca yacate. abhayam sarvabhutebhyo dadamyetad vratam mama" \[Valmiki Ramayana VI: 18:33\], "mamekam saranam…sarvapapebhyo" \[Bhagavadgita [XVIII:66](https://vedarama.com/en/books/bg/18.66)\], "vaisamyanairghrnyena sapeksatvat". The statement "Visnu is sleepy", is against the statements that describe Visnu in the meditative sleep ("yoganidra"). The statements "sarve nimesajjajnire vidyutah purusadadhi", "aditya rbhavosvapna", etc., describe only ordinary gods as not having any sleep. The statements "yoganidram jahau harih", and "nidramudram nikhilajagatiraksane jagarakam" in the middle, describe Visnu in the meditative sleep.

The argument "The ten Saiva puranas are sattvika and the four Vaisnava puranas, namely, Visnu Purana, Bhagavata Purana, Garuda Purana, and the Narada Purana, are tamasa," is also against the proof of direct perception. Even the Padma Purana describes the greatness of Visnu. The statement "astabhih kathyate…" is against the statement in the Padma Purana, which mentions that six puranas are about Visnu.

Now, the statement "visvadhika umapatih" mentions that Siva is the best of all deities, is the goal of knowledge sought in the Vedanta, the giver of moksa. That description is also contradictory in meaning. Thousands of our treatises have properly argued that the srutis, smrtis, itihasa, and the puranas do not describe Siva as surpassing the universe, as the giver of liberation, or as the goal of knowledge sought by the Vedanta. These texts describe Siva as an individual soul and Visnu as the best among all the deities, the giver of moksa, and the goal of knowledge sought by the Vedanta. Your commentators also describe God with attributes as the best among all, the giver of moksa, etc. They do not mention Siva as the giver of moksa, etc.

Here, we have not discussed that topic out of fear of diffusion. The statements "yatha vedesu-", etc., are not authoritative because their meaning is wholly refuted by the srutis, sutras, smrtis, itihasa, and the puranas. Therefore, the division of the puranas into sattvika, etc., which is described in those statements, should not be accepted. Only the division of the puranas, which is described in the Padma Purana, etc., and which is favoured by the srutis should be accepted. The tamasa puranas such as the Matsya Purana, etc., are not authoritative. Those puranas are composed for praising Siva and, therefore, their meaning is not true. Hence, Siva cannot be comprehended with the term "narayana" on the basis of the etymologies given by the statements in those puranas.

Moreover, consider the words "sukla" (white), etc. There are many usages in which such words are seen to refer to the subject that possesses those qualities. The lexicon, whose sole purpose is to determine the power of signification of words, mentions that the words "sukla", etc., signify the quality of whiteness, as well as the possessor of that quality, ("gune sukladayah…"). However, the terms "sukla", etc., are understood as signifying the quality itself because of the brevity in signifying the meaning of quality. Specific (i.e., not generic) qualities do not signify the possessor of that quality because possibility of such interpretation is cumbersome. That meaning, which is understood exclusively from a word, is the meaning of that word. Otherwise, the power of indication would be destroyed.

The logicians said that in understanding the words such as "saindhava", etc., there is no alternative but to understand the word "saindhava" as having separate powers of signification in the sense of horse and salt. When it is possible to explain the usage by resorting to the power of indication, it is not right to imagine many meanings. And, therefore, the word "saindhava" does not refer to the possessor of the quality with its power of signification. "Saindhava" refers to "horse" with the power of indication. The primary power of signification refers to the quality only. The lexicon intends to show the meaning established with the power of indication.

The grammarians also intend to show that the words "sukla", etc., refer to the quality (of whiteness, etc.) with their power of signification. That is why they said that the suffix "matup" (in the sense of "possessing") after the words denoting qualities is elided in the words, which denote "the possessor of that quality" ("gunavacanebhyo matupo lugistah").

Our partisans also accepted that the words that signify adjectives, which are not separately established, may refer to the "qualified" by extension, but discarded the idea that the signifying power of each word may be extended to refer to the "qualified".

Your partisans also explained the separation of the terms for quality ("suklah": white) and the possessor of quality ("patah": cloth) in expressions such as "suklah patah" (white cloth) by recognizing that the signifying power of all words does not point to the "possessor of that quality". Although "the act of acquiring" and that, "which is being acquired" reside in the same locus, and are identical in the sense that their existence is not separate because they reside in the same locus, the "quality" and "that, which is qualified" are different.

Thus, even the lexicon, whose sole goal is to determine the power of signification of words, does not accept that one can comprehend the "qualified" with words possessing many powers of signification. "Qualities" are different than the "qualified". The term "narayana" is not seen as referring to Siva in any places other than the laudatory statements you cited. Such use is not seen in the stories, puranas, srutis, or in the worldly usages. Therefore, the names "narayana", etc., cannot be understood as referring to anyone else. Even if one argues that the passages specifically describing the ultimate Brahman are about the Supreme Brahman, your story about comprehending Siva from the term "narayana" on the basis of the etymologies you cited is certainly discarded.

It is not justifiable to explain that words have more than one meaning. That is why all the Purva Mimamsakas unanimously say that the term "prsta" refers to Rathantara, etc., with the power of indication. The term "prsta" is established as a name of a laudatory hymn in the Citradhikarana. In some places such as "kvacit prstairupatisthate" it is used in the sense of Rathantara with the power of indication. The laudatory statement in the sruti "apo va rtviyamarcchan tasam vayuh prsthe vyavarte", tells about the reason for using the term "prsta" to refer to Rathantara. This laudatory statement becomes meaningful by explaining the above use of "prsta". The Purvamimamsakas determined that the term "prsta", which is established as a name of a "stotra" in "na rathantaradau", refers to Rathantara with its power of indication.

Thus, in the matter about a usage in a sruti, explanation in the heard laudatory statement determined that the term "prsta", which is established as a name of a "stotra", is used in the sense of Rathantara with its power of indication.

Similarly, the term "narayana", which is established by the lexicons as the name of Visnu only, may be understood as referring to Siva with its power of indication, and not with its power of signification. Experts in Mimamsa should know that it is not possible to assign many primary meanings to words, because it is cumbersome.

Siva is the subject of discussion of derivations in the passages of the Saiva puranas that are in praise of Siva, and are cited by only you. Only you understand him as referred to by the term "narayana" in the laudatory passages from the cited Saiva puranas.

Besides, the Manu Smrti uses the term "narayana" to describe Visnu because He abides in the causal waters ("asididam tamobhutamaprajnatamalaksnam. Apratarkyamavijneyam prasuptamiva sarvatah. Apa eva sasyadau tasu viryamapasrjat. Tadandamabhavaddhaimam sahasramsusamaprabhamam. Apo nara iti prokta apo vai narasunavah tayadasyayanam pumstena narayanassmrtah"). The Manu Smrti was composed for providing true answer to the query about how and from whom the universe was born. It is proclaimed as the most authoritative text ("yadvai kimca manurava dattabhdesajam": Whatever Manu said, that is medicinal). It seems that even the statements that you yourself cite ("visnussvayambhurbhagavanu") describe Visnu as abiding in the causal waters. One cannot describe two gods as the cause of the universe. This Manu Smrti is well known and is quoted by your partisans such as Appayya Diksita. Any smrti that is against the meaning propounded by Manu is cut down ("manvarthaviparitattu ya smrtissa sasyate"). If in conflict with the Manu Smrti, another smrti becomes ineffective. If in conflict with a smrti, purana becomes ineffective. If in conflict with the Manu Smrti, a purana will be completely ineffective. If in conflict with a sattvika purana, the tamasa purana is ineffective. Merely laudatory parts are especially ineffective. Thus, the passage you cited ("sarvanamnam gatirvisnureka eva janardanah. Na tu narayanadinam namnamanyatra sambhavah. Rte narayanadini namani purusottama. Pradadanyatra bhagavan rajevarte svakam puram") is from a tamasa purana. It is in conflict with other puranas. It can be otherwise established as being in praise of Siva. It is completely ineffective because it is in conflict with the Manu Smrti, which is pre-eminent over all. Therefore, the term "narayana" cannot be understood as signifying Siva. The term "narayana" does not have the signifying power to refer to Siva on the strength of the above passage.

There are two statements: "agrayanadgrhnati", and "ukthyannirgrhnati". Agrayana: the first soma libation at the Agnistoma sacrifice; Ukthya: a libation at the morning and mid-day sacrifice. Sodasi: one of the Samsthas of the Soma ceremony; Samstha: the basis or essential form of a sacrifice.

Now, a problem arises: Sodasi can be taken from both the Agrayana and the Ukthya. Then there is a statement from another school: "tam parancamukthyat sodasinam grhnati". In order to avoid any conflict with this statement, the Mimamsa determined that the above-mentioned two statements should be understood as "one takes sodasi from agrayana after the ukthya").

Similarly, for recognizing the excellence of the Manu Smrti, here also, one should understand the description of Siva as abiding in the causal waters in the following manner: Siva is not different from Narayana, and, therefore, is described as abiding in the causal waters. Once it is established that He is not different from Visnu voiced by the term "narayana" because He abides in the causal waters, it should be noticed that this etymology does not establish that the term Narayana signifies Siva. You may say, "Narayana is called "narayana" because He abides in the waters that are produced. Therefore, the Supreme God cannot be called 'narayana'." From the statement you cited, one does not comprehend that Narayana abides in the produced waters. What it says is "adbhih krtam": made by the waters. Even if Narayana is described somewhere as abiding in the produced waters, it does not hurt his description as abiding in the causal waters (according to the nyaya "adhikam tu pravistam taddhani". Besides, we do not see any statement that negates Narayana's abiding in the causal waters.

Besides, a question arises: is Siva, who is one of the three deities, the intended object of signification by the term "narayana", or some other Siva? Not the first one. Thus, in the passages you cited, there are statements, which describe the Supreme Brahman as the impeller of Brahma, Visnu, and Mahesvara. There are statements, which describe the root that releases the seed, which is the supporting stem of the First Creation. God is described as "One". As mentioned in the Janmadyadhikarana (Brahma Sutra), He is perceived as Creating, Preserving, and Destroying under the sway of the gunas. He is described as the First God, Unborn, Support of the multitudes of naras (individual souls), Grantor of Liberation, Independent, Great God, Brahman, higher than all, Destroyer of all, Omnipresent. He is referred to with the term "bhagavat" (blessed), and with the sound 'om'. These are the signs of the Supreme Brahman. These will be refuted if they are understood as describing one of the three deities. The second alternative is also not acceptable. First you asserted that the term "narayana" refers to the three deities, and then say that it refers to God other than the three deities. This is incongruous.

One should not say, "Siva, who is among the three deities is identical with the Supreme Brahman and is, therefore, described with the adjectives that describe the Supreme Brahman."

Such an interpretation would conflict with the chapters that describe the greatness of One God, other than the three deities. The above passages mainly describe Siva, who dissolves the universe at the urging of Parama Siva. He is not understood as identical with the Supreme Brahman. Therefore, such an interpretation cannot be justified.

One should not say, "The term 'narayana' in the cited passages signifies Siva, who is other than the one among the three deities. It is in the form of superimposition of 'Siva' among the three deities on the other deities."

Even if there is superimposition of one on another, it is known that the term "narayana" signifies only Visnu, and therefore, it cannot be established that it signifies Siva other than the one among the three deities. On the contrary, it establishes that the term "narayana" signifies Visnu only. The Brahman beyond the three deities is without any attributes and cannot be expressed with words. One cannot establish that it is expressed with the term "narayana", which is based on etymology. The Supreme Brahman cannot be described as having the properties that are expressed with the etymology. All the properties described in the statements become nullified in the case of the Supreme Brahman. One would have to understand the statements as describing something that is not real, or merely praising the Brahman without any attributes by indication. We would not grasp any meaning as signified by the term "narayana". And all the examples will be futile.

One should not say, "Even if the term 'narayana' does not signify Siva, who is among the three deities, let it signify Siva with attributes, who is other than the three deities."

There is no evidence that Siva is the one, who is above the three deities. It is possible to say that Narayana is the one who is real ("srstisthityantakaranim brahma visnusivatmikam sa samjnamyatibhagavanekaevajanardanah. Brahmavisnusiva brahman pradhana brahmasaktayah").

One should not say, "Here, the term 'Siva' is used to signify the Brahman that is beyond the three deities. Therefore, all the terms such as 'Narayana', 'Siva', 'Brahma', etc. signify Him only."

There is no purpose in imagining that the terms signifying the three deities signify the Brahman, which is beyond them. This also will cause conflict with the lexicons.

One should not say, "Even if we decide that there are additional meanings other than the ones mentioned in the lexicon that does not negate the meanings listed in the lexicons. Therefore, there is no conflict. Besides, there is a purpose, namely, description of one deity with the terms 'Narayana', 'Siva', etc., in the Vedas."

There would be a problem in establishing that your "Attributeless" is the goal of knowledge of Vedanta. You accepted that the puranas are of three kinds. Your position would be rejected if we understand the puranas as being about the Brahman beyond the three deities. That also would refute all the statements, which describe the puranas as of three kinds. If we accept that with the power of indication, the "Attributeless Brahman" is the goal to be sought in the Vedanta, it is possible to explain that the terms "narayana" etc., which are established by the srutis, etc., refer to the "Attributeless Brahman" with their power of indication although they refer to each of the three deities with their power of signification. Therefore, it would be futile to accept that all the terms signifying the three deities signify a deity with attributes that is beyond the three.

In the other view, if the above explanation is not accepted, it is possible to explain that each term among the terms "narayana", "Siva", etc., refer to one particular deity with the power of indication. If the above explanation is accepted, the terms "narayana", "Siva", etc., refer to the individual deities with their power of signification. Thus, it is possible to explain all the Vedanta scriptures as being about their particular desired deity. However, in your view, all terms refer to the "Attributeless Brahman" with the power of indication. There is a fault in your reasoning that power of indication is required to understand the meaning of all the terms. Such use of the power of indication is refuted in your own learned tradition. Besides, there will be a problem that the "Attributeless Brahman" would not be understood as the goal of knowledge in the Vedanta.

Therefore, since it is not possible to understand the terms "narayana", etc., as referring to anyone else other than the three deities, one should understand them as praising the "Attributeless Brahman". Thus, the term "narayana" cannot be understood as signifying Siva. Therefore, it is futile to cite those passages.

This is the summary.

**Conclusion**

The etymology, i.e. the first and the second etymologies contain many references, which describe the signs of the Supreme Brahman. The third etymology also describes the signs of the Supreme Brahman as seen in the corrective statement "sambassatyadilaksanah". The second etymology states: "naranamayano yasmattena narayanah smrtah". The Supreme Brahman is in all forms. This property is secondary in the second etymology. In the first etymology also, according to the "agrayanadgrahananyaya", one should accept the meaning that is in harmony with the Manu Smrti, etc. Thus, there is comprehension of the Supreme Brahman in all the three etymologies. Therefore, they cannot be understood as referring to Siva among the three deities and the meaning accepted by you cannot be established.

Now, in our view, Narayana is the Supreme Brahman. The term "narayana" signifies Narayana alone. If, as you suggested, the terms "narayana", etc., are understood as referring to the Supreme Brahman, the etymological meaning would be refuted. Therefore, those references should be understood as being in praise of the Supreme Brahman. Therefore, there is no chance that the term "narayana" signifies the Supreme Brahman. Your Appayya Diksita himself has many times refuted the view that the etymologies and usages establish the meaning with their power of signification. And even if, somehow, it is understood that they establish the meaning with their power of signification, the statement "na tu narayanadinam namnamanyatva sambhavah" specifically states that the terms "narayana", etc., cannot be used in any other sense. Other faults also have been pointed out previously. Therefore, the etymologies you cited do not establish the meaning with their power of signification. Besides, they do not have the authority because they belong in the tamasa puranas.

After examining conflict with the Mimamsadhikarana, the terms "narayana", etc., have been explained as signifying Siva by citing your etymologies and usages. However, because the term "purusa" is about Narayana, while considering the Purusa Sukta, the Vedic etymology says, "purvamevahamihasamiti tatpurusasya purusatvam" (I was here before, that is the purusa-hood of the Purusa). In the etymologies in the puranas ("purusamajne sarire'smin sayanatpuruso harih. sakarasya sakaro'yam vyatyayena prayujyate"), in general statements in important puranas ("bhagavaniti sabdo'yam tatha purusa ityapi nirupadhi ca vartete vasudeve sanatane", "anyatra hyupacaratah rudhya vakti janardanam"), in Vedic usages such as "purusadevatya" in Puranic usages such as "yasmacca madhye purusah parasmat", and in the examples cited by us the terms "narayana", etc., do not have power of signification to refer to Siva. Appayya Diksita composed this treatise involving the Mimamsadhikarana because the terms "narayana", etc., do not have the power of signification to refer to Siva.

You should not say, "The term 'purusa' reminds one of Narayana. Therefore, it signifies Narayana."

It is not established that the term 'purusa' especially signifies Narayana. The Abhidhanakosa says, "purusavatmamanavau". ("The word 'purusa' means the soul and a human being.") There are many such usages of the word "purusa" in the Vedic as well as non-Vedic literature. Therefore, it is necessary that we understand it as signifying the Self. It can be explained that the term "purusa" is used to refer to Visnu because of that. There is no reason to imagine that the term "purusa" refers to Visnu with its signifying power. The cited statements can be explained as laudatory because there are many references to the qualities such as total independence, etc., of the "purusa". Therefore, the term "purusa" does not have the specific power of signification to denote Visnu on the basis of usages and etymologies. This topic is not discussed here completely because of fear of diffusion. You should see it in the "karnamrta", etc.

Thus, the etymology given as cited by Appayya Diksita is in conflict with many places in his books, is not seen in his old books, and is not cited by our partisans. Therefore, one should regard it as an insertion by the modem, not so learned writers. Even after accepting that the specific usages, which contain the above etymology in his treatise, do not signify Narayana with their power of signification. We have established that the term "purusa" refers to Narayana.

Similarly, you should be satisfied that the term Narayana in the usages and etymologies you cited does not have the power of signification to signify Siva.

Moreover, you mentioned that the term "purusa", which occurs in many usages in the srutis and important puranas, in many specific statements from the important puranas, in the etymologies in the srutis and important puranas, does not have the power of signification to signify Narayana. Then the term "narayana", which appears in the usages and etymologies you cited and which are in the laudatory sections in the tamasa puranas that are in conflict with the sattvika puranas, important srutis, smrtis, itihasas, etc., do not have the power of signification to signify Siva. This is established with the Kaimutikanyaya ("the rule of 'how much more?' or how much less?' arguing a fortiori"). \[Monier-Williams Sanskrit-English Dictionary, p. 311, col. 2\].

Thus, Narabhojalayana says, "narayanastvam na hi sarvadehinam. atmasyadhiso'khilalokasaksi": ("You are Narayana. No, you are the soul of all, the Lord, the witness of the whole world.") There are many etymologies such as "tesamayanabhutatvat…" ("Because He is their support, their refuge"), "narajjatani tattvam naraniti vidurbudhah" ("the elements were born from Nara. That is why the wise- men know them as 'naras'."). In those statements, Narayana is described as immanent in all, the witness of all, the cause of the whole world, and the giver of liberation. You accept this. The author of the Brahma Sutras ("sutrakara",) determined that those are the properties of the Supreme Brahman. Your commentators also said the same thing. "Being the cause of the universe" is the sign of the Supreme Brahman. This description applies to Narayana alone. This is naturally understood from the statements in many Upanisads, which contain the terms "narayana", "visnu", "Vasudeva", etc. For example, "eko ha vai narayana asinna brahma nesanah", "narayanad brahmo jayate", "narayanad rudro jayate", "visnustadasiddha…va niskalah". In the chapter on the Immanent God ("antaryamiprakarana"), it is naturally understood that the description of immanence in all, which contains the term "narayana", applies to Narayana alone ("esa sarvabhutantaratma apahatapapma divyo deva eko narayanah'). Visnu appears to be of the "all-pervading" nature of the Supreme Brahman ("antarbahisca tatsarvam vyapya narayanah sthitah"). He is the one who is described as the best of all, the soul of all ("visvatah parmam nityam visvam narayanam…"). In laudatory statements such as "mata pita bhrata nivasah saranam suhrt", "gatirnarayanah…", He is described as the refuge of all, the friend of all, the goal to be attained, which is the nature of the Supreme Brahman. Then there are statements from the lexicons such as "krsnonantah kesavo munjakesah", and usages such as "anantam avyayam kavim…" \[Mahanarayana Upanisad XI:7\], the term "ananta" is established as the name of Visnu. The statement, which investigates the nature of the Supreme Brahman, contains the term "ananta" with the accusative declension. Absence of distinction caused by the threefold component (the three gunas) and omniscience ("brahmano vipascite") is the very nature of the Supreme Brahman. Visnu appears to be of that nature. He is understood to be the Grantor of Liberation ("tamevam vidvan amrta iha bhavati", "rudram tu sarathikrtva moksamicchejjanardanat"). This is the nature of the Supreme Brahman. These descriptions of the Supreme Brahman cannot be understood as referring to two deities. All this is agreed upon. You yourself also have not accepted Siva as the Supreme Brahman whether the Siva is one of the three deities, or someone else. We have discussed all this at length many times. We are not writing about it here out of fear of diffusion.

The etymology "naranamayano yasmat" (from whom beings originate), etc., is among the etymologies you cited. Such etymologies indicate that Siva is described as of the nature of the Supreme Brahman, and then understood as meant by the term "narayana". All the properties of the Supreme Brahman are described in all the sruti statements, which contain the terms signifying Narayana. According to our view, He is the Supreme Brahman. No one has accepted that both deities can be the Supreme Brahman. Even the Saivites describe that Visnu is the immanent god and is the material cause. Your partisans also have not accepted that Siva, either from among the three deities, or some other deity, is the Supreme Brahman. Your own reasoning refutes the meaning of the etymologies you cited. One should accept that they are not authoritative because they are from the tamasa puranas, or they are meant to be in praise of Siva. Therefore, those statements, on the ground of their being true statements, cannot establish that the term "narayana" signifies Siva. Now, it is enough discussing a small matter in too many words.

You did not cite any etymology in support of the view that the term "narayana" signifies the four-faced god. You only cited a passage from laudatory chapters. That too appears in the chapter lauding Brahma. The chapter is in praise of "non-existence" ("asadarthavada"), which refutes the argument about the cause of the universe, etc. That passage cannot lead to the comprehension that the term "narayana" signifies Brahma. Even if, somehow, you assemble some etymology, it would be proven otherwise according to the methods discussed before. Therefore, it would also not lead to comprehension that the term "narayana" signifies Brahma. Therefore, according to the lexicons, etymologies in the Puranas, specific statements, and Vedic and non- Vedic usages, the term "narayana" is specific to Visnu alone. When we determine that the term "narayana" refers to Visnu, its analysis as "naram jivasamudaya ayatyenam" ("naram" = multitude of the individual souls, returns to him) is not appropriate. The statements from the Santi Parva and the Padmottara Purana, which you cited contain a form with the genitive case ending. Therefore, it should be a compound involving the genitive case. That would not be the compound desired by you. And the compound you are making would not be correct because it is against the grammar.

Now, you may interpret the term "narayana" as follows: "He comes to the state of an individual soul for sport." This interpretation is also inappropriate. The lengthening of the initial vowel would not be possible in this interpretation. Also, the etymology would require us to understand that the focus is on the abstract member of the compound ("the state of being an individual soul"). And it is not derived in that manner. It is not correct to introduce a difficult interpretation when it is possible to interpret it as a bahuvrihi compound, which naturally follows the etymology.

You may say that the word "narayana" can be split as "naram = jalam ayati ("He reaches 'nara', i.e. water"). Here the initial vowel is long in the stem itself. This would explain the word "narayana". The term is not connected to "narayana", who is a god.

The above argument is also not valid.

Thus, there is a statement in the tamasa Siva Purana cited by you: "taya saha jale svapan". This statement uses the form "jale", which is in the locative case in the sense of locus. It suggests that the term "narayani" is formed with a bahuvrihi compound. Actually, the statement contains the word "saha" (i.e. "with") in "taya saha". It suggests that the term "narayani" is connected to "narayana". Panini Sutra "pumyogadakhyayam" \[IV: 1:48\] states that the suffix "nis" is added to a noun-stem when it is connected to a man. And the grammar states that this connection with a man is not necessarily in the form of a husband and wife. Connections other than that of a husband and wife are also intended when the connection with a man is mentioned. Therefore, it is possible to derive the form "narayani". It is natural to understand the word "narayani" in this sense because of the phrase "taya saha" (with her) in the statement without having to resort to a separate derivation like "jale svapanti" (they sleep in the water).

Thus, there is no derivation of "narayani" as "naram jalam ayati" (the one who resorts to the water). The statement you cited naturally shows a connection with a man. Therefore, you cannot deny the connection with Narayana. It is also incorrect to say that Visnu attained the state of an Individual Self for pleasure. The state of an individual soul leads only to sorrow. It cannot lead to pleasure. And an independent person cannot possibly deliberately proceed towards one's own ruin. Therefore, one cannot explain that Narayana comes to the state of an individual soul. If you say, "The sorrow is unreal ('mithya')", there is nothing undesirable. Then why would anyone make an effort to abandon the state of an individual soul and become liberated? In the passages you cited, we hear about Siva and the Brahman without any attributes becoming individual souls. Visnu has not been mentioned as becoming individual soul. Accordingly, it is not correct to say that Visnu becomes an individual soul. If the term "narayana" were understood as denoting the meaning "He reached the state of being an individual soul", it would be a name of the individual soul, as the terms Siva, etc., are. It would not be a name of Visnu. Even though, as you say, the Brahman itself becomes "jiva" (individual soul), the word "jiva" does not refer to Brahman with its signifying power. Similarly, the term "brahman" does not refer to "jiva" with its signifying power. All words are established according to the difference in their conditioning factors.

Besides, according to your view, "isvara" is the one who is reflected in "maya" and "jiva" is the one who is reflected in "avidya". Visnu is included in the category of "isvara" as the reflection of pure consciousness. Therefore, it is not appropriate to consider Him to be an individual soul.

According to our view, Siva is understood as bom from action. Srutis such as "anahatapapma va ahamasmi nama me dehi", "narayanadrudro jayate", etc., support our view. Even when understood as the individual soul, Visnu is understood to incarnate at will. Various authoritative statements support this understanding. (For example, "apahatapapma… narayanah", "ajayamano bahudha vijayate", "tasya dhirah parijananti yonim", "janma karma ca me divyam", "na mam karmani limpanti", "icchagrhitabhimatorudeha", etc.) One does not sense his birth from any action. One comprehends him to be the original cause through statements such as "eko ha vai narayanasit", etc. And it has been established in many ways that the individual soul is not separate from the Brahman. Besides, He is not the individual soul. Therefore, to say, "Visnu becomes the individual soul", is against the views of both of us.

Now let us look at the etymology of "narayana" as "the child of Nara, the Supreme Soul", or "the child of Nara, the sage named Brahma". This etymology also should be discarded. Even when your quotations denote the meaning you mentioned, that meaning does not derive from them. Also, the sages have given the derivation of the term. Only those explanations should be accepted. The word "nara" in its general sense does not signify the sage named Dharma. It may communicate such special meaning with its power of indication. The views that hold that the dative case termination affixed to the noun "nara" is not elided, are incorrect. When there exists a derivation given by the sages, that one should be accepted. It is not possible to understand the term "narayana" as a compound in which a case termination is not elided without willful interpretation. And in this particular case, a compound with a frozen case termination is not good. Compounding a noun with the dative case termination is limited to where there is mutation of the original form or state. The words "caturthi tadartharthabalihitasukharaksitaih" from Panini Sutra II:1:36, states that a noun with dative case termination may be compounded with the stems 'artha', 'bali', 'hita', 'sukha', 'raksita', and with a noun stem in the sense of 'for it'. The sense 'for it' means the compound is formed when the mutation of the original form or state is the intended meaning. For example, "kundalaya hiranyam" = gold for the earring = "kundalahiranyam". Here, "hiranya" (gold) changes into the earring. When no such change occurs, there is no compounding. For example, "randhanaya sthali" = a pan for cooking. There is no mutation and hence no compound. In the example "ahalyayai jarah" = lover of Ahalya, the form "ahalyayai", which has a dative, singular case termination, is a Vedic form. Therefore, you cannot cite it as an example.

And asvattha is a manifestation ("vibhuti") of Narayana. It is possible that narayana could be a name of "Asvattha". But when Narayana is a name of Visnu, there is no purpose in imagining that Narayana is "Asvattha".

The argument that the nameless and formless Supreme Soul receives the names Narayana, etc., through contact with maya, is also not correct. From the srutis such as "namarupe vyakaravani", "namarupe vyakarot", etc., we understand that the phenomenal world, which is a form of prakrti and the names that conform to the forms were created. But God is not a form of prakrti and is eternal. This is established with many proofs. The rise of such names and forms of prakrti is not impossible. That is why it is necessary to say, "He is without any names or forms, i.e., He is without any forms of prakrti and without any names that conform to those forms."

Now, let us consider the statement "mayayogena pancadhavasthitah" ("He remained in five-fold forms through contact with the maya.") Here also, the mention of five forms is in conflict with many proofs, which assert that the Brahman is eternal and not a product of prakrti. It is not possible to understand God's nature as a modification of maya, which is a form of the prakrti. According to the statement "mayavapusamjnanam" in the Vedanighantu, and according to "icchagrhitabhimatorudeha", the word maya in "mayayogena…" is certainly about His free will. Both the examples you cited are connected with maya and, therefore, cannot serve as proofs in making determination about the term Narayana. If you accept that He is under the sway of maya, there will be a conflict with the description of Vasudeva as eternal and pure in the same example ("vasudevo dhruvah suddhah"). It would mean that the term Vasudeva refers to God in his pure, eternal form, and He is called Narayana because of His contact with maya. Thus, the statement, in the sense in which you cited, becomes like a rope you yourself brought to bind yourself.

It is inappropriate to say that the mantra with eight syllables ("om namo narayanaya") is a product of prakrti because it has white colour ("sasuklavarnamonkaram…"). Just as the Vedas are not the products of prakrti because they are eternal, the mantra with eight syllables, which is a part of the Vedas and describes the eternal God, also cannot be a product of prakrti. It is at the root of the manifestation of names and forms in the world by God ("namarupam ca bhutanam krtyanam ca prapatcanam vedasabdebhya evadau devadinam cakara sah"). Since there is no form in sound, for the facility of worshipping, the mantra with eight syllables is mentioned as "white". The term "narayana" is not a product of prakrti. This is a pointer.

Now, about your argument that the term "narayana" refers to Siva: You say, "He becomes 'nara', i.e., a human being for the protection of the world ". That explanation is also inappropriate. The initial vowel would not be lengthened. The suffix 'lyut' is enjoined after a verb to form an abstract noun, in the sense of an instrument, or locus. \[Panini Sutra III:3:114, 115, 117\] Examples: "hasanam" (abstract noun) = laughter or the act of laughing; "palasasatanah" (instrument) = palasa tree-cutting instrument; "saktudhani" (locus) = vessel for barley meal. The suffix 'lyut' is not enjoined in the sense of an agent. Therefore, the word "narayana" cannot be explained as formed with the suffix 'lyut'. Hence, the word "narayana" would have to be explained as formed by adding the suffix 'an' "karmanyan" \[Panini Sutra III:2:1\]. When a preposition, particle, etc., prefixed to the verb is its object, the suffix 'an' is affixed to the verb. For example, "kumbhakarah" = maker of jars. Here, "kumbha" = jar is the grammatical object of the verb "kr" = 'to do'. Here, the suffix 'an' is affixed to the verb "kr" = 'to do'.

One should not say that the suffix 'lyut' in the sense of an agent is possible because the rule Panini Sutra III:3:113 uses the term "bahulam", i.e., variously applicable. \[Panini Sutra III:3:113, "krtyalyuto bahulam": the suffixes known as 'krtya' and 'lyut' may be used in senses other than those enjoined.\] This use arises out of helplessness. When it is possible to understand the term as the well-known bahuvrihi compound, one should not seek another explanation.

One should not say, "While explaining the Bhagavata Purana verse 'narayanastavam na hi sarvadehinam', the commentator comments on the phrase 'sarvalokasaksi' in that verse. It is explained there that the phrase is about the derivative meaning 'naram ayati' = knows the whole world = Narayana. Then it is explained that the term 'narayana' is formed in the sense of 'naram ayati' by applying the rule 'gatikarakopapadanam krdbhih saha samasavacanam prak subutpatteh'. The Lingabhattiya also gives the same explanation ('naram ayati')."

The sage means that Narayana is the Supreme Brahman and the term "narayana", which is preeminent among the terms denoting the Supreme Brahman, contains all the properties that specifically describe the Supreme Brahman and describes them with the signifying power of its components and with inference based on the circumstances. Thus, some characteristics are described with the tatpurusa and bahuvrihi compounds. The phrase "adhisokhilalokasaksi" is used with the intention of describing Him as the Lord and the Witness with the signifying power of its components. It is impossible to avoid the futility of those two adjectives without assigning such intention. According to the rule "aksepatah prasadabhidhanikasyaiva grahyatvat" (One should accept the lexicographical meaning over the meaning arrived at by hinting.), it is appropriate to understand that the term "narayana" means to describe His characteristics with the power of signification of its components. Although the term "narayana", which is famously used to refer to Narayana, can be understood as being about Visnu ("naram ayati") with the rule "gatikarako…" out of necessity, the rule "gatikarkopa…" is occasional. It is not applied in the usages such as "dharmakrita", etc., but is applied in the usages such as "asvakrita", etc. out of necessity. It cannot be applied to interpret the term "narayana" in the sense of Siva. There are no well-known accepted usages in which the term "narayana" refers to Siva. In order to refer to some special subject, one cannot freely assign a bad meaning, which is contrary to the sastras, to a word when it has another well-known meaning. The examples you cited ("sadyassyandamanah…, samudrayanah…" etc.) are not subject of the rule "gatikarakopapadanam…» The retroflex 'na' in those words can be explained because they are bahuvrihi compounds and a name has been given to them. Or, the retroflex 'na' in those forms is due to their being Vedic forms. Therefore, those forms cannot be used as examples here. Thus, the rule "gatikarakopapadanam…" does not apply here. One must say that these are bahuvrihi compounds, etc. The power of signification of the etymologies you cited has been refuted many times. The sutra "purvapadat samjnayam agah" \['Panini Sutra VIII:4:3 says that the dental 'n' changes into the retroflex 'n' if 'r' or 's' is in the previous word. There is no change if the consonant 'g' intervenes. The change mentioned in this sutra takes place only in the case of names.'\] Does not apply here because we are talking about purely etymological meaning. Therefore, the dental 'n' cannot change to the retroflex 'n'. Besides, according to the rule "usage supersedes the derivation" ("rudhiryogamapaharati"), there is no possibility of the term "narayana" being in the sense of Siva. Therefore, this whole effort is futile.

The argument that the term "narayana" is derived as "the male offspring of the Sage Narada, i.e., 'Silada'" is also not respectable. The cited statement only says that Siva was born as Nandi. There is no derivation of the term "narayana". There is not even a hint that it is a proper name. The rule "rudhiryogampaharati" (custom supersedes derivation) refutes it.
