And "narayana" would have to be understood as the name of Nandi. Since there are differences in conditioning factors, one should speak of only the established usages. Otherwise, names of Nandi also would have to be understood as names of Siva. Besides, the word "nara" does not signify the Sage Silada. If words that have general meanings are interpreted as having some special meaning, common nouns such as "caitra", "putra", etc. also can be interpreted as having special meanings. Then, there would be no rules.

The next interpretation is as follows: "'naram' = 'gangajalam'= water of the Ganga; he abides in it. That is why He is Narayana." It is also inappropriate. The word "nara" does not signify "the water of Ganga". The purely derivative meaning does not explain the change from dental 'n' to retroflex 'n'. It also does not explain why the word is in masculine.

It is also inappropriate to understand the term "narayana" as signifying Brahma and explain it as "naram = jalam ayate: he goes to 'nara', i.e. waters." The cited statements only describe Him as abiding in the waters. There is no etymology. And even if there were an etymology, it would not refer to Brahma with the power of signification because of the defects mentioned earlier. Purely derivative meaning would be eclipsed by the faults mentioned earlier.

Then there is the interpretation: "Stems of the lotus coming out of His navel form his paths of coming and going". It is also not good. The cited statement does not give the derivative meaning of the term "narayana". There is not even a hint that it is a name. And the change of the dental 'n' into the retroflex 'n' would be unexplained. Besides, to imagine that the sounds 'r' and T are identical and therefore, interchangeable, is a subject matter of colourful puns. You cannot use that argument in our context because the context is about the statements by sages.

Words such as "nalikera", etc., cannot serve here as examples because those are Vedic words. Therefore, it is totally inappropriate to say, "The term 'narayana' is common to all the three deities." The term "narayana" is very specific to Visnu alone. The lexicons, etc., support this.

Now, let us consider the argument that "all names may be used to refer to all three deities because all the three deities are reflections of one consciousness. It is possible to use the names of all three deities to refer to each of them." This argument is also nice until considered. In this view also, you cannot avoid problems such as the lack of arrangement of all the characteristics mentioned by the Puranas, etc. Besides, our opponents have not accepted that the three deities are reflections of consciousness. You cannot treat it as accepted. Therefore, the argument that "all names signify all the deities," cannot be used to refute the view of our opponents.

Moreover, it is inappropriate to argue all three deities are reflections of one consciousness. The statements and derivations you cited speak of Siva as having the signs of the Supreme Brahman, as being the Creator of Brahma, Visnu, and Mahesvara, as being Immanent in All, as assuming the human form for sport, as the cause of the whole universe, etc. They speak of Visnu also as the giver of liberation and immanent in all beings, which is the description of the Supreme Brahman. They describe Brahma also as the Lord of the Universe, which is a sign of the Supreme Brahman. Thus, there is a conflict with your own treatise, which describes Siva as the Supreme Brahman.

Besides, a question arises: are the three deities reflected in maya, or in avidya? The first alternative is not correct. Your doctrine says, "Isvara is the reflection of consciousness in maya." You argue in your book that Visnu is an individual soul. If you accept the first alternative, there would be a conflict with this argument. The second alternative is also not correct. Your doctrine says, "individual soul is the reflection of consciousness in avidya." If you accept the second alternative, there would be a conflict with your treatise, which maintains that Siva is not an individual soul by describing it as the Creator of Brahma, Visnu, and Siva, and by describing it as assuming a human form for sport.

Moreover, even if one were to accept that the three deities are reflections of one consciousness, a question arises: do you accept the three deities as different according to the difference in conditioning factors or not? The first alternative is not acceptable. Even if the individual souls are reflections of consciousness, you will certainly have to say that because of the difference in the conditioning factors, the names of Siva cannot signify Visnu and the names of Visnu cannot signify Siva. For example, an individual, whose name is Caitra, cannot be referred to as Maitra, and vice versa.

The second alternative is also not acceptable. If you were to say that they are not different, the usage that they are "three" is inexplicable. Our scholars have many times refuted the doctrine of "bimbapratibimbabhava" (the condition of the original and its reflection) with arguments such as: (1) It is not possible that a formless can reflect into formless; (2) Since there is no difference between the original and its reflection, the arrangement of "with attributes" and "without attributes", "ignorant" and "omniscient" would not be possible. And they gave different, unrefuted meanings of the examples you cited in support of your arguments. They refuted the meaning, which you desire.

They also refuted the doctrine that there is no difference between the individual soul and God, and explained with proofs the difference between the individual soul and God. They properly justified the view that the phenomenal world is real by many times refuting the view that the phenomenal world is unreal. Our scholars have properly refuted the "avacchedaka" view also. We have not written down all of this because the book would be too long.

Even if you argue by resorting to the "avacchedaka" view, the same faults, which we mentioned while criticizing the "bimbapratibimbabhava", are seen in the "avacchedaka" view also. So, do not hope to succeed by resorting to that view. The followers of Vivarana and Bhamati have refuted the views "bimbapratibimba" and "avacchedaka", which are accepted by Vivarana and Bhamati, themselves.

Therefore, we have to say that the names denoting each particular deity signify only that particular deity. Therefore, the argument that each deity may be referred to by the names of all three deities is not acceptable. This is established.

Salute to Sri Ramanuja.

## Appendices

## Appendix 1 - A Note on Vijayendra Tirtha and Appayya Diksita

When the Muslim Bahmini Sultanatesdefeated the Vijayanagar Empire in the Battle of Talikot in 1565 CE, many scholars and artists who had received patronage by its emperors, fled to safety into the Tamil country. The Nayaks of Tanjore and Madurai, who were initially the feudatory vassals of the Vijayanagar emperors, later became independent spin-off kings after the defeat of that empire. These Nayakates became the new protectors of Hinduism in the deep south of the subcontinent.

During the reign of Sevappa Nayaka \[1532-1580 CE\], the Nayak of Tanjore, three savants graced his court. They were: Vijayendra Tirtha \[1514-1595 CE\], Kumbhakonam Tatacarya \[1545-1630 CE\] and Appayya Diksita \[1520-1591 CE\]. Sevappa Nayaka regarded them as the three jewels of his court. Vijayendra Tirtha was a Madhva-Vaisnava, Tatacarya was a Ramanuja-Vaisnava, and Appayya Diksita was from the Sivadvaita School of Saivism. There used to be many triangular debates among these scholars quite frequently. The three scholars would ask Sevappa Nayaka to choose between them. However, Sevappa Nayaka would diplomatically reply that he could not choose one as he liked all three of his jewels.

Once when a dispute arose at Kumbhakonam (Tamilnadu) between the priests of the SarangapaniTemple who were Ramanuja-Vaisnavas and the Saivite priests of the Kumbhesvara Temple over the exclusive use of the common temple tank called Hematirtha Puskarani, the two parties went to Vijayendra Tirtha to arbitrate over the dispute since he was not only the senior-most scholar but a well-respected one as well as a member of a totally different sect than the other two. The Ramanujites are extreme Vaisnavas who do not worship any deity in the Siva- parivara. Similarly, the type of Saivites who were in charge of the Kumbhesvara Temple at that time was extreme Saivas under the leadership of one Emme Basavendra. These extreme Saivas like their Vaisnava counterparts did not worship any deity in the Visnu-parivara. The Madhvas though Vaisnavas by sectarian affiliation, have no qualms about worshipping any deity in the Siva-parivara. So, it was quite understandable that the two quarrelling parties came to the illustrious Madhvite abbot for arbitration.

Vijayendra Tirtha ruled that the temple tank should be shared equally by both parties. However, the Saiva leader Basavendra displeased with the ruling is said to have put stone-lingas and basava-images (both of which are Saiva symbols) into the tank so that the Ramanujites would not be able to use it. The Ramanujites it is said complained to Vijayendra Tirtha who went to the tank and is said to have uttered mantras. The temple tank then yielded salagrama-stones and cakra-images (both of which are Vaisnava symbols). Hence, Vijayendra Tirtha again ruled that both parties should use the tank. The tradition is that the Ramanujite priests of the Sarangapani Temple were so grateful to the Madhvite abbot that they allowed the Madhvites to do the daily naivedya puja at the Sarangapani Temple which purportedly continues to this day.

Vijayendra Tirtha was named as "Vitthalacarya" by his parents. He became an ascetic and was ordained under the monastic name of "Visnu Tirtha". He studied under Vyasa Tirtha \[1478-1539 CE\], the brilliant scholastic-doctor of the Madhva-Vaisnava tradition as a whole, and the abbot of a Madhvite matha. When Surendra Tirtha, the abbot of another Madhvite matha, came to Vyasa Tirtha to ask for an able successor, the latter "gifted away" Visnu Tirtha to him. Surendra Tirtha renamed Visnu Tirtha as "Vijayendra Tirtha". Upon Surendra Tlrtha's departure from this world, Vijayendra Tirtha became the abbot of that institution in 1539 CE. He remained so for over half a century until his departure from this world in 1595 CE. His mortal remains lie enshrined at Kumbhakonam \[Tamil Nadu\].

Vijayendra Tirtha wrote many works. Some of these are:

1\. Commentaries on the 10 original treatises of Madhva  
2\. Commentaries on the three original treatises of Vyasa Tirtha  
3\. Madhvatantramukhabhusana \[a polemical work defending the tenets of Madhvaism against the criticisms of it by his rival Appayya Diksita\]  
4\. Paratattvaprakasika \[a polemical work upholding the supremacy of Visnu over Siva as the Supreme Being and a reply to the counter-claim made by his Saiva rival Appayya Diksita\]  
5\. Bhedavidyavilasa \[a polemical treatise defending the concept of difference and a criticism of the Bhedadhikkara of Nrsimhasrama, the Advaitic polemicist who criticized the doctrine of difference\]  
6\. Siddhantasarasaviveka \[a polemical treatise criticising the doctrines of Ramanuja-Vedanta and Sivadvaita\]  
7\. Cakra-mimamsa \[a treatise defending the Vaisnava (Ramanujite and Madhvite) practice of branding one's body with the accouterments of Visnu such as the cakra and sankha. It provides evidence from a wide variety of scriptural sources such as the Vedas, Pancaratra Agamas, Puranas, etc.\]  
8\. Pistapasu-mimamsa \[a treatise upholding the use of dough shaped as animals for use in Vedic sacrifices instead of actual ones\]  
9\. Saivasarvasvakhandanam \[a polemical treatise refuting the supremacy of Siva over Visnu depicted in the various episodes of the Saiva Puranas\]  
10\. Subhadradhananjaya \[a drama depicting the love and wedding of Krsna's sister, Subhadra, to the Pandava prince, Arjuna\]

Appayya Diksita was named "Vinayaka" by his parents. He studied under one Rama-kavigal and soon became a great scholar in his own right. He was patronized by the Nayaks of Gingee as well as by the Nayaks of Tanjore. He too wrote many works. Some of these are:

1\. Siddhantalesasangraha \[a treatise that summarizes the works of all the Advaitic scholastics\]  
2\. Kalpataruparimala \[a commentary on the Kalpataru of Amalananda which was a commentary on the Bhamati of Vacaspatimisra which in turn was a commentary on the Brahmasutrabhasya of Sankara\]  
3\. Caturmatasarasangraha \[a treatise summarizing the doctrines of the Madhvite, Ramanujite, Sivadvaitic and Advaitic traditions based on their respective commentaries of the Brahmasutras\]  
4\. Madhvatantramukhamardana \[a polemical work demolishing the tenets of Madhvism\]  
5\. Ramanujamatasrngibhanga \[a polemical work demolishing the tenets of the Ramanujite tradition\]  
6\. Taptamudravidravanam \[a treatise severely decrying and demeaning the Vaisnava (Ramanujite and Madhvite) practice of branding one's body with the accouterments of Visnu such as the cakra and sankha\]  
7\. Sivatattvaviveka \[a work upholding the supremacy of Siva over Visnu\]  
8\. Sivarkamanidipika \[a commentary on the Brahmasutras from the Sivadvaita standpoint\]  
9\. Ramayanasarasangraha \[a commentary on the Ramayana extolling Rama as an instrument of Siva and refuting the Ramanujite commentaries on the epic\]  
10\. Mahabharatasarasangraha \[a commentary on the Mahabharata from the Saiva viewpoint wherein the Bhagavadgita supposedly enunciates the supremacy of Siva\]  
11\. Bhasmavadavali \[a treatise extolling the greatness of the sacred ashes and their wearing of it\]  
12\. Sivapuranatamasatvakhandanam \[a work which refutes the view that the Siva Purana is a tamasic work\]

Aksobhya Tirtha \[r. 1317-1365 CE\]

Adil Shahi Sultanate of Bijapur, Band Shahi Sultanate of Bidar, Imad Shahi Sultanate of Berar, Nizam Shahi Sultanate of Ahmednagar, and Qutb Shahi Sultanate of Golconda.

Sarangapani means "the holder of the Saranga bow" which refers to Visnu. This word ought not to be confused with Sarangapani meaning "the holder of a deer" which refers to Siva. The word "Sarangapani!" (Siva) is more common than the word "Sarangapani" (Visnu) in India. Indians, who are not learned in the sacred lore of Hinduism, often confuses "Sarangapani!" for "Sarangapani!"

The title is particularly caustic as it translates to "breaking the horns of the Ramanujite tradition". It is an oblique reference to the sectarian forehead markings of the Ramanujites.
